ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-15.ana.ud176e170	: The Presidency today puts forward a position on the regulation of the remuneration of the depositors and the members of the government. The Presidency promotes a unanimous proposal that the Storting should set the remuneration in line with the recommendation of the Enlarged Bureau in its position. The Commission has obtained information from several teams to monitor wage developments in Norwegian working life, both from private and public activities. The Commission refers to the agreement on the level of settlement of the year. As in previous years, the Wage and Occupational Pensions Commission has focused on the expected annual wage growth at the time of the year’s settlement. The position of the Wage and Wage Commission represents an annual wage increase in 2017 for the grossing-up representatives and members of the government of 2.4 pst. It is based on calculated annual wage growth in both the state and municipal as well as private sectors. The Presidency points out that the Wage and Wage Commission will highlight that developments in recent years, including: higher unemployment and lower oil prices have contributed to moderate wage settlement. The Presidency further notes that, although it is the Commission’s view that the remuneration of the government’s members should be adjusted over a period of time in order to improve compliance with comparable government management salaries, the Commission considers that 2017 is not the right time to increase the remuneration beyond the average annual settlement framework. This is the Presidency's agreement. On this basis, the recommendation of the Presidency is recommended.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-15.ana.ud176e183	One might say that the staging representatives go from summer party to payroll party. Initially, I would like to address the presidency's position and the proposal of the remuneration committee from 1 May 2017. As the Commission itself writes: ‘In the Commission’s 2016 position, the Commission pointed out that a gap has developed over time between wage developments of central government executives and central government representatives and government members. This is also confirmed in statistics ordered by the Commission from the SSB in 2015. It is therefore assumed in the 2016 exit that the survey shows a lower wage trend compared to the employees of the government’s management wage regulation. [[Directors, Heads of Mission and Councils of Departments...]] The Commission further writes: ‘In this position, the Commission pointed out that it considers it natural that the remuneration of the Prime Minister and members of the Government, respectively, should remain at such a level as to reflect the responsibilities of the members of the Government at all times. The Wage Commission is of the opinion that it is not intended that this group has fallen so far behind the wage trend of the leaders of the country and that this less-wage development should be corrected in the long term.» In a way, I fully agree. Few people in this country bear a greater responsibility than the Prime Minister, and it should also be reflected in the status of the Prime Minister and also in the determination of wages. Just to shed some light on the development of society in some important areas for which the Storting has a major responsibility, I would like to draw up three examples of income development, for three groups. The one is women, the other is farmers, and the third is fishermen. It is a fact – it has been declared by the Stortinget in previous treatments – that we have a gender-based labour market today, where women in the women-dominated professions feel significantly less than in the men-dominated. Women's pay is now 87 pst. of the wages and salaries of men, measured as differences in hourly wages and salaries. Until the mid-1980s, there was a sharp decrease in the hourly difference between women and men, but in recent decades, developments have remained largely silent. The reasons for gender differences in hourly wages are many and complex. Gender differences in pay are highest among two groups: the skilled and those with a high level of education. Men with vocational training are far better off than women with vocational training. This will include, for example: explained that male-dominated vocational training typically leads to better paid jobs in the private sector, while female-dominated vocational training leads to poorly paid jobs in health care and education in the public sector. At high levels, we typically find men in high-wage jobs in the private sector and women in relatively poor paid jobs in the public sector. In fact, even when we look at relatively similar women and men, hourly differences between women and men rise higher in income distribution. So, they're getting higher returns on their investment in education in the world of work across the payroll, but mostly at the top. Around a quarter of the pay gap between women and men who are equally well educated and of the same age may be attributed to differences in wages and salaries between industries, but there are also differences in wages and salaries within industries. Finance industries have the highest hourly wage differentials, where women only receive 70 psts. men's wages are paid for the same type of work, responsibility and education. There are also factors other than each one on the labour market that have a bearing on the current wage gap. Children still mean relatively much for hourly differences between women and men. The hourly difference between mothers and fathers is more than twice the difference in hourly pay between women and men without children. While women are re-entering men when it comes to education and professional experience, when women and men with the same human capital and family situation are compared, the pay gap is still present and the pay gap in occupations and industries seems to be increasing. Why do we have this situation? This is a reality description that has been read and adopted, but still continues. Well, because our collective bargaining system is organized so that it keeps the pay gap up. The negotiating system is structured in such a way that it is the so-called front disciplines that negotiate first and premise the wage negotiations in other industries. Frontal education, or the emerging industries, is largely dominated by human beings. As a result, the wages of women-dominated industries are to some extent related to the fact that they are not to drive wages further up in competitive industries. For this reason, it has been pointed out repeatedly to the UK Government and the UK Government that the pay gap will never be close if the government does not take the initiative to expand the framework for the women-dominated groups, i.e. to be willing to increase the salaries of these professions beyond what is negotiated in the field of primary education. ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-15.ana.ud176e641	I would first like to thank the members of the Committee for their good cooperation in the examination of this communication. Avinor’s societal mission is to own, operate and develop a nationwide network of civil sector airports. In addition, they have a single civil and military air navigation service. Avinor shall initially be sea funding, whether there are two exceptions to Bodø airport and new airport at Mo in Rana in the coming NTP period. Avinor is currently responsible for 45 airports following the closure of Narvik airport. The Avinor model is a co-financing between profitable and unprofitable airports. In 2016, eight airports had a positive operating result, while the remainder, i.e. 38 airports, had a negative operating result. Of Avinor's income, approximately half from fees. The remainder comes from commercial revenues, about half of which are from free sales at airports. The revenues from the major airports, Oslo, Stavanger, Bergen and Trondheim, are essential for maintaining a profitable airport network and the way we have organized it. A Joint Committee supports Avinor’s objective to further develop Oslo Airport as a national and international hub. Avinor believes that Oslo Airport will reach the capacity limit around 2030 and that a third runway will then be needed. It is important that Avinor exploits today's capacity and today's runways as far as possible with the use of technological solutions for approach and departure management and, where appropriate, physical measures before taking this step, but the majority of the Committee, consisting of the Right Party, the Advance Party, the Christian People's Party and the Workers' Party, supports Avinor's continuing to plan a new third runway and that the Eastern option be used as a basis for the further process. A minority of the Centre Party and the Left do not want further work to be done on a third runway. Avinor has been through major investments, not least with new terminals at Oslo airport and at Bergen airport, which will be opened later this year. Avinor has several projects in progress, such as: extension of terminal capacity at Stavanger airport. There is also a need to expand the capacity of Trondheim Airport in the near future and to expand the terminal at Tromsø Airport. The air navigation service shall invest in a new en route system platform. Furthermore, we must invest in the new remote-controlled tower technologies. An overall committee refers to the NTP period 2018-2029, where funds have also been allocated for the transfer of Bodø airport and the development of new airport at Mo i Rana. With regard to the Mo-Rana airport plans, a Joint Committee has asked the Ministry of Transport to continue its work in cooperation with Avinor with the project with a view to finding a sound financing solution as soon as possible. This must be done in close cooperation with local and regional actors. The Committee also takes the view that no changes are being made to the current airport structure in Helgeland in the construction of a new large airport in Mo i Rana. This means that Lake Mosque Airport Kjærstad is not laid down in the construction of a new airport at Hauan, as previously provided by Avinor. The Committee also welcomes the continuation of Avinor’s plan to invest in new Hammerfest airport and any new airport in Lofoten. Avinor works with remote towers. Between 2017 and 2020, 15 of Avinor’s airports will move to remote-controlled towers, which will then be remotely controlled from Bodø. I would like to add that a majority in the Committee agrees to this, but believe that further competitive outsourcing of remote-controlled towers should be awaited until their impact has been assessed. It is positive that Avinor has clear objectives of being a driving force in the environmental work of aviation and focuses on minimising the environmental impact of its own activities. It is important that this work is given high priority at all aerodromes. It is also important that Avinor is a driver in order to facilitate the provision of public transport to and from airports as much as possible. As regards the further competitive deployment of critical functions such as fire, rescue and ground services, the majority of the Committee considers that experience from Bodø Airport should be gained and the evaluation of Bodø should be awaited before any new competitive outlook is made.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-15.ana.ud176e909	The Avinor model has served Norwegian aviation and travellers well for many years. ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-15.ana.ud176e1161	When the world changes, frameworks and rules need to be modernised as development progresses. There are not many good examples of positive developments in those areas where the industry has not had an incentive for customer-adapted development. The taxi industry has lacked the tools to improve its industry. Public rights regulations prevent centres from putting higher standards of quality and content in the product at the best of consumers. Public regulation of the taxi market has long been mature for revision and modernisation. The legislation is now very fragmented, and the responsibilities are unclear. The Competition Authority concludes that: ‘The day’s regulation of the taxi market does not facilitate effective competition and productivity growth.’ And on the other hand, «Not least technological developments have led to a change in the basis for price regulation in the taxi market.» At the same time, the taxi industry reports a great need for change. We shall not prohibit services in order to protect existing business models, but the Right wants to ensure equal treatment between the different actors. At the same time, the changes need to open up new technologies and facilitate innovation and productivity-enhancing measures. The market economy has become the focus of the debate on the sharing economy. The Divisional Economics Committee proposes to suspend the rights regime for taxis. One of the main concerns is lack of competition in the taxi industry. However, one has to distinguish between the order market, which is the largest part of the taxi business, and what is going on in the street. There is a high level of competition in the order market, not least in the public sector. When you take a cab from a taxi stop, you're given the cab queue. As in most strictly regulated industries, an unregulated market arises on the outside of the system and at the same time entities in a grey zone. Pure acts of piracy are carried out to a large extent and create problems both for security and for the regulated industry. Companies like Uber arise in what is today a grey zone. Transport activities appear to be organised here without payment of service, thus clarifying the formal rules of the law on the payment of transport services. In principle, one has to assume that the conditions are very different between the densely populated urban areas and the rest of the country. In scattered rural areas, public procurement will be the foundation for the provision of a taxi service at all. On the other side of the scale, the Oslo area is located, where there should be spaces for several competing centres. It is a paradox that in Oslo and Akershus, where prices are to be set in a market and where there are several taxi centres, prices are increasing. All nutritionists must be subject to the same laws and regulations. Whether the company is named Oslo Taxi, Bergen Taxi or Uber, there are the same requirements for reporting to the tax authorities and for complying with laws and regulations relating to users' rights and society's requirements for road safety etc. whichever is applicable. The industries that are now being challenged must be given the opportunity to strengthen their position by developing – at the best of their customers. In many places you live too far, or we simply have so many individual travel destinations that buses do not meet all needs. The cheese market is very different from place to place. In Oslo, Bergen, Trondheim, Stavanger and other major cities, it is relatively easy to get hold of taxis for what you want, and there is relatively strong competition between the players. Out in the districts, on the other hand, there will be longer between the taxis, and it will take longer before the taxi gets where you are, to drive you to where you are going. Making sure that you have a functioning market, which, after all, offers the best possible offer, and which is able to meet the needs you have for both work and leisure, is also an important part of the offers needed to ensure school transport, either on a fixed basis some places or when a child breaks a foot and is a bit dependent on extra help. Because we know that in some of the countries that have been heavily liberalised, there has been an even better and cheaper offer in the cities, but we have also experienced that people can no longer live on it as the only profession, and that has made it possible for some places in the districts to have a worse offer. At the same time, we know that in Norway we stand out a little from the fact that a lot of the taxi service in the districts has contracts with, for example, the public and carry out many ordering tasks, which they know ensure a certain amount of income. Modern technology in Norway would not have the consequences that have been seen elsewhere, simply because the taxi market has a different income base at the bottom. Here's a lot of different questions you have to answer. IP addresses and subscriber data can help reveal criminal activity. Data protection today calls for the storage of IP addresses and subscriber information for up to three weeks for billing purposes, but there is no legal requirement for such storage. Demonstrating that the storage of IP addresses will actually have an impact on the fight against serious crime, and what consequences this will have for the privacy of people in general, is absolutely central if one is to legislate it. Since the Data Storage Directive was being processed, the storage of IP addresses was implemented. Later, the directive was, as you know, lifted because several courts considered it too far to impose the retention of sensitive information. One consequence of this is that the statutory storage obligation has not entered into force. The mass storage of personal information is problematic, but the storage of IP addresses is initially not metadata storage. The storage of IP addresses is more in line with the storage of phone numbers. Therefore, this becomes more legal than a technical question. Digital development is fast, and these changes make it necessary to ensure targeted actions at any given time. Since 2001 terrorist attacks have introduced a number of measures that increase the level of monitoring in society and laws and regulations that have reinforced penalties and instruments. In recent times, we have also deployed more resources for operational services, and cyber coordination centres have been established to strengthen potential to address threats in the digital space. Our security requires increased coordination between public security actors, and it is from the government that proposals will be made for a ‘digital border defence’. Today’s use of the Internet brings with it complex problem-solving options, with the need for careful evaluation of the most crime-removal action. There is a need for an approach that both takes care of health and looks at the long-term effects. The security of each and every one of us is, as I said, a core task for the state, and an important part of our security is also to ensure a good balance between the rule of law, privacy and surveillance.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-15.ana.ud176e1335	Technological developments have been enormous in a few years, and the Internet has become a good thing that we absolutely need to protect. Unfortunately, this case is not a unique individual case. According to the head of the Oslo Police District section for sexual offences, Kari-Janne Lid, only the top of the iceberg is revealed. I note that the majority of the transport committee knows that the police have called for an extension of the storage obligation for IP addresses, because it could strengthen the police's ability to fight serious crime, as the proposal is based on. However, all parties, other than the Christian People's Party and, to the extent also the Advance Party, do not choose to support the proposal of the Christian People's Party to ensure a storage time of IP addresses for up to six months. The representative Michaelsen says they want more than six months. The important thing for the Christian People's Party has been six months, as the police have said will be sufficient, and we promoted that proposal for hopefully bringing us the Right Party or the Workers' Party on it. It is important to be careful and sober in these matters. Different considerations need to be weighed against each other in order to ensure a proper balance between privacy considerations and the need for resolution of serious crime. It is important that storage is not disproportionate and that the duration of storage is sound. In addition, it is necessary to assess the severity of the offences likely to be remedied or resolved through storage and the likelihood that serious crimes against innocent people may be averted or resolved. The Christian People's Party opposed the implementation of the Data Storage Directive – something to the right and the Workers' Party was not – precisely because it would result in disproportionate interference with individual privacy. The proposal we are now promoting is something quite different and far from being as radical as the Data Storage Directive would be. The Data Storage Directive required providers of electronic communications to store all data traffic for a period not less than six months and up to 24 months. The directive on the storage of data traffic in general involves the storage of much more extensive information on the communication and movement of persons than only the storage of subscriber information on IP addresses. It involves information on electronic communications, on the use of subscribers, inter alia: the telephones that have been in contact with each other by SMS or by telephone, the duration of the call, the location data for communication equipment and the use of IP addresses. This was also illustrated by the Data Protection Authority in the context of the Stortinges processing of the Data Storage Directive in 2011, where they supported the storage obligation for subscriber information related to IP addresses, even though the Data Protection Authority was opposed to the other parts of the Data Storage Directive. The representative Michaelsen also quoted the Director of the Data Protection Agency, Thon, who pointed out that there was a significant difference between this and DLD. Therefore, the arguments against the Data Storage Directive cannot be fully used against the proposal that we are discussing today. Subscription information related to IP addresses is comparable to a telephone number. It does not reveal any content and only allows for the identification of which subscriptions are made to the individual address for which period of time. However, this information may be crucial in situations where the police become familiar with pictures or videos of child abuse, shared online, and which give reason to fear that children in the material are being subjected to ongoing abuse. Then the storage of IP addresses can allow the police to search for victims, possible perpetrators and perpetrators. Data protection currently accepts the storage of IP addresses and subscription information for up to three weeks for billing purposes. But it has turned out that this is too short a deadline for the police to investigate serious net crime. In particular, international cases make it difficult to reach it within three weeks. The Christian People's Party therefore believes that it is time to give the police the tools they are asking for, so that we can better protect our kids. I take up the proposal we have made in the vote, and I warn you that we are going to vote subsidiaryly for II.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-15.ana.ud176e1585	A growing period goes to the end, and a lot has happened since the fall of 2013. And thank you to the entire committee for the cooperation we have had on Norwegian foreign policy in the four years that have gone by. In debates like this one today, it is rare to conclude that there is broad consensus on the broad lines of Norwegian foreign and security policy. We have tradition of continuity and consistency. Norway shall be able to rely on, across changing governments. We often say that Norwegian foreign policy is firm. It shouldn't. When the world changes, we must be prepared to change, also politically. It has been eight years since the last time the main lines of Norwegian foreign policy were addressed in a wholesale communication. St.meld. No 15 for 2008-2009, Interests, responsibilities and opportunities, were presented in spring 2009. The Communication of Interest, and the previous work on the basis of a comprehensive public discussion, report and book publishing on Norwegian foreign policy, were thematically broader than the present message, with its clear emphasis on security policy. Professor Iver Neumann, one of our leading foreign policy experts, has pointed out how the 2009 notification of interest constituted a distinction in Norwegian foreign policy thinking by relaunching the concept of interest in Norwegian foreign policy, unlike a more traditional emphasis on identity. It is important to note, then, that a unanimous committee expresses its view that the conclusions of the Communication in 2009 are still valid, including in the context of a changed security policy situation. One should perhaps be careful about using metaphors from the missing time of the paper lexicons, but the present message appears to be a complement to the 2009 message of interest – a security policy supplementation. Since 2009, significant changes in the field of security policy have obviously occurred. In our own neighbourhoods, the agreement with Russia on the sharing line in the Barents Sea in 2010 constituted a milestone – in a positive sense – in Norwegian foreign policy history. At the same time, Russia’s Crimea Inspection from 2014 and the destabilisation of eastern Ukraine in a negative sense constitute the most serious challenge to the security policy order in Europe after the end of the Cold War. Our neighbourhood to Russia must be based on cooperation and dialogue, firmness and predictability. We must join our allies and European partners in the meeting of violations of international law. But in a neighborhood or a branch, you have to have a good relationship with and open communication with all of your neighbors, and the most important neighborhood relationship is with those you share fences with. Russia is not just a country in our neighbourhood; Russia is our neighbor. We share borders, and not least we have an unconditionally negotiated frontier at sea. Norway has interests in maintaining contact with Russia, including in difficult times. The ban on foreign policy debate should be taken care of, and I am probably not the first to dwell on the concept of 'road elections'. But through this message the terms "options" or "to choose" are, in practice, only found in the reference to the title of the message, not in the contents. At the same time, the term ‘recurrent’ occurs 32 times, ‘continuing’ 41 times, and ‘strength’ not less than 92 times. Nevertheless, road choices may also be made by continuing and ending. For example, the Professor Neumann mentioned here. called for the closure of Barents' cooperation, as it has become rhetorical swada. I disagree. It is in Norway’s long-term interests to fend for cooperation in the north, laboriously built up and an important contribution to the low voltage in the north. I referred to the message as a security policy supplement. Another suitable metaphor may be the ‘Live message’, for the message provides a concise and concise description of the changes in the security policy landscape. This may also be the reason for the absence of fundamental disagreements in the Committee's position. It's not very controversial to agree on what the loop conditions look like. The message is also succinctly concise and concise, but the list is quickly long over the themes and angles you would like to see more thoroughly illuminated and processed. China is one example. The Committee has been particularly concerned with raising the importance of China’s growth and probably the most important geopolitical shift in our time. China's significance is nothing new. China has been the world's largest economy in 18 of the last 20 centuries. Today, however, China is also becoming an increasingly important player in almost all areas of foreign policy and security policy of importance to Norway. In assessing Norway’s role in the world, it is possible to go into the ditch on both sides. Our importance can be overestimated, and it can be underestimated. I would warn you here against the latter conclusion of error, to underestimate our foreign policy significance. We are not a small country either. Take our economy: Of almost 200 countries in the world, Norwegian economy is in 25th place. Our contributions to the UN, including in absolute terms, make us a great force. We're not a spectator to the world either. We are an important player, for example. When we go ahead and engage in dialogue with groups that other countries are not talking to – such as the Myanmar military junta, the FARC GSR movement in Colombia and the moderate forces in the Afghan Taliban. Norwegian peace diplomacy is an important and proper priority. Let it also be said that a Norwegian role in peace and reconciliation is fully possible to combine with being a strong ally, that we are participating and doing our – also military – to combat terrorism and the causes of terrorism. Unpredictability has become indicative of the time we live in. Then it is also timely to conclude here with a reminder that the course of world history does not take account of the negotiating pace of Norwegian wholesale communications. On 11 August 1989, Minister for Foreign Affairs Thorvald Stoltenberg presented a wide-ranging report on developments in the international community and the impact it would have on Norwegian foreign policy. For three months later, on November 9, 1989, the Berlin Wall fell. But here there is also a lesson for Norwegian foreign policy in 2017. It is precisely in changing times that new opportunities and scope for important initiatives are opening up, such as: when, in the early 1990s, Minister of Foreign Affairs Thorvald Stoltenberg took the initiative to establish this Barents cooperation.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-15.ana.ud176e1619	Let me begin by joining in the committee chairman's mention of the work of the committee during this period, and then it is also natural from this pulpit to thank the committee chairman, Huitfeldt, for the management of the committee during the period. It is important to pursue the consensus policy that has given us sustainability in Norwegian foreign policy over very many years, even under changing governments. The policy message is an important and timely message, which I would like to welcome. The values underpinning our foreign and security policy are firm, but the world is changing rapidly. The climate of security policy has changed markedly in recent years, and the world is more unpredictable. We must therefore take on board the fundamental changes that are taking place, with international unrest and deep crises in the Middle East and North and West Africa. We question the standards and the liberal world order we built after World War II. The Roadmap Communication discusses how Norwegian foreign policy will meet these challenges. We need to have a strategy to build the choices we make under Norwegian interests and Norwegian security, and we need to make clear choices. The key message is that Europe's southern neighbourhoods are in crisis. The rules of international law, which have served Norway, are under pressure and European cooperation is under test. In parallel with these challenges, we see shifts of power in a world that is becoming ever more densely woven together. In the background are common challenges with significant inherent potential for conflict, such as climate change and resource scarcity. There are many positive developments and success stories around the world. The number of poor people in the world has fallen dramatically in the last 25 years. Nevertheless, the recent turbulence and acute crises have given a sense of increased vulnerability. Three factors characterise the situation in the world today: increased complexity, unforeseeableness and transparency. We need to be able to deal with these factors. Russia’s annexation of Crimea in the spring of 2014 and the destabilisation of eastern Ukraine changed the security policy landscape in Europe. Europe is united in its support of the responses to the Russian violations of international law against Ukraine. Norway has concluded on EU restrictive measures by introducing its own equivalent measures. However, for Norway, it is important both to safeguard its own interests in the practical cooperation with Russia and, at the same time, to stand firm on international law and to defend important principles of international relations. The Roadmap Communication highlights the need to further develop cooperation with Russia based on common interests and a firm, predictable policy. The tension between Russia and the rest of Europe is taking place in the Western Balkans, where we are seeing a worrying development. The emergence of terrorism and violent extremism is a serious development. ISIL, the worst terrorist organization in our time, has been controlled in a large geographical area, and we have seen the influx of foreign fighters and a influx from Europe to fight for these terrorist wars. It's a pleasure to see that they're now on a partly waving front. Norway maintains significant civil and military contributions to the anti-ISIL coalition, and as a result of this and the coalition's efforts, ISIL is severely weakened in Iraq and Syria. The number of African countries and victims affected by terrorist attacks has increased significantly over time. Groups of soluble Al Qaida and ISIL have found support in North Africa and in the belt from Sahel to the Horn of Africa. In 2015, 9 African countries were on the list of the 20 countries in the world most affected by terrorism. Boko Haram was the same year the world's second most deadly terrorist group after ISIL. The terrorist groups in the region are also threatening the south of the Sahara and Europe. The challenges in Africa and the Sahel Belt, the Levant and the Maghreb, with vulnerable countries at risk of collapse, will affect us in Norway and Europe. It is therefore important that the government will increase its efforts in Europe's unstable neighbourhood by implementing a strategy for Norwegian action in vulnerable countries and regions. Increased Norwegian assistance to the unstable areas of the Middle East, North Africa and Sahel is an instrument to prevent the collapse of vulnerable states. We sometimes hear criticism of setting up some of the Norwegian aid against vulnerable countries, so here it is important to remember that some of the most marginalised people and groups live in these countries. There has been low economic growth in Europe over several years, although it is on the rise, but we are seeing a worrying development in Turkey with increased polarisation. Brexit is leading the second largest economy and the strongest military force in Europe to leave the EU. Increasing nationalism, protectionism and populism are a ‘toxic cocktail’. The policy message is based on a pro-active approach to these challenges. It is right to further develop the long lines of Norwegian foreign policy by contributing to the preservation and further development of the international legal order and strengthening the UN and other international organisations. Norway shall work to promote human rights, the rule of law and democracy and respond to serious violations of international law. Institutions to safeguard human rights and democracy have a more important role to play. We must work to protect and strengthen multilateral institutions and promote democracy, human rights and the rule of law. The Norwegian OSCE Presidency is now working in 2020 and Norway is elected a member of the UN Security Council for the period 2021 to 2022. NATO must be armed with the challenges we face. Norway is NATO in the north. The government will work for closer cooperation between NATO and the EU. Europe must take greater responsibility for its own security. It is good that the government will launch security policy dialogues with selected allies: France, the Netherlands, the United Kingdom and Germany. It's important to maintain American commitment in Europe, because the United States is our most important ally. We must therefore maintain the strong transatlantic ties and further develop the long-term security policy cooperation we have with the United States. President Trump recently confirmed that the United States is committed to transatlantic cooperation and NATO solidarity through Article 5. It's a pleasure. We have a long tradition in Norway with close cooperation with changing governments in the United States. It is positive that the government will contribute to conversion in NATO, so that the collective defence of the Alliance is strengthened against both old and new security challenges. At home, the long-term plan and the strengthening of our national defence are an important response to the challenges we face. The long-term plan represents a historical economic strengthening of the Defence. It is necessary after many years of underfinancing. The long-term plan contributes to national strengthening of our national defence and is a direct contribution to strengthening NATO's defence capabilities. We make heavy investments in submarines, maritime patrol aircraft, new fighter aircraft, air protection, large army investments and intelligence, to mention something. The government is delivering on strengthening Norwegian defence capabilities and facilitating increased allied presence and exercise in the north. Norway has taken initiative in NATO's maritime strategy, and it is crucial for us with sea areas six times as large as our land areas with a strategy affecting the North Atlantic and our nearest land areas. These areas are becoming increasingly strategically important in the military. Norway has also initiated a review of NATO’s command structure. The vision of a world without nuclear weapons is an expressed goal for NATO. At the same time, the alliance states that it will have nuclear weapons as long as such weapons exist. Nuclear disarmament must be balanced and reciprocal. A unanimous increase on 26 April 2016 decided that Norway should actively work for a world free of nuclear weapons and contribute to fulfilling the obligations of the non-proliferation agreement.

There is no doubt that the world around us in recent years has been more troubled. It is war in the Middle East, we have seen extensive terrorist attacks and conflict in Ukraine. This means that Norway must now show the will to build up its defence, and we must have a broad discussion of security policy. The central party wants a society where the people control the evolution of the country. A strong rule of the people in Norway has led a well-functioning and sovereign nation state. A strong and well-functioning nation state has maintained a strong defence and that we secure our national independence. I am pleased that a joint committee determines what could have been taken straight out of the Central Party's party programme, namely that Norway's national interests must be at the forefront of Norwegian security policy. The message is based on a slight lack of understanding of security and foreign policy and perhaps lacks an analysis of measures for some of the more defined global developments in the world today. Members of the Committee, including members of the Centre Party, stress the importance of increasing inequality, migration and climate change for our own security. A missing person in this message gives an indication of these dimensions and a reflection of how it should be viewed in our security policy options. Increasing inequalities, as we see in many countries, migration and climate change are drivers of conflict, and increasing inequalities within countries can lead to destabilization and stagnant political and social development in countries. Security policy must also be seen in the context of development policy. This can make the message somewhat ambitious on Norway's behalf. As the case-speaker said, Norway is not a small country in this context. And Norway should take more than less responsibility in areas where we can make a good contribution. We need to have an active and not a reactive foreign policy, and we need to take advantage of the space we have. We have changed our respect in many areas. Examples of issues where Norway should be more able to take a leading commitment are nuclear disarmament, where unfortunately this government has not been leading, conflict-building work, where it has been done and done very well, and reforming the UN peacekeeping forces. This is all the fields where we have a bit of power, but where we can strengthen our commitment. A joint committee states that at the same time as the Norwegian security policy throughout the post-war period has been based on collective defence through the NATO alliance, it has been Norway's line since we entered NATO in 1949, that we do not allow bases for the future powers to base their battle forces on the Norwegian territory in peacetime. The Committee of Consultants also notes that it is in Norway's interest to maintain stability and low tension in the neighbourhood. The Central Party will stress that the Norwegian basic policy has been a pillar of Norwegian security policy since Norway joined NATO in 1949 and that the basic policy is still to be established. It is important at a time when this question is being debated and there is some uncertainty created by the government about what a real meaning means.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-15.ana.ud176e1870	There is a broad consensus that the security policy situation is more serious than in the past, not because no direct threats have arisen to Norway, but because unforeseeableness has increased: Crimean annexation, eastern Ukraine, terrorist attacks, cyberattacks — in some attempts to dismantle the institutional order in Europe, well assisted by right-wing populist movements that will withdraw their country from a committed community, a United Kingdom leaving EU cooperation, and an American president breaking down with completely central support bars in American foreign policy over the past 70 years. After World War II, the United States took the lead in building important international institutions such as the World Bank, the IMF and NATO, and in Europe, EU cooperation was built – in total an institution-based political order. This changed the thinking about the concept of national interests. Community considerations became a new and increasingly major factor in international policy. Later, the impact of globalisation also emerged and created a new and strong economic integration between countries. Cooperation on a rule-based trade regime was emerging, today organised in the WTO. This institution-based political order developed a democratically anchored cooperation culture between countries. Conflicts could be dealt with within a new, peaceful and democratic arena. In this development, the United States has played a very central role, not only as a security policy guarantor as the leading global military power and the leading NATO country, but also through what we can call an idealistic foreign policy. The United States saw itself as a militant for and defender of liberal democratic values — not always as perceptive and successful in meeting liberation forces in other countries, and not always as consistent in the choice of partners, especially under the cold war definition of what was right and wrong, or during today's abuse of religion as a definition of conflict. But the American self-image and leading star was never «America first». It's the new one from a country that's been important for our security and for the international institution-based order. This greatly contributes to the unforeseeableness in which we now stand. In this situation, it is reassuring that EU countries strengthen cooperation in the field of security policy and defence policy, not as a competitor or substitute for NATO, but as a complement. A global strategy for EU foreign and security policy is in the process of being launched, and this week the Commission launched a reflection note on the development of defence cooperation, while launching the defence fund for research, procurement and development funding. Norway has the opportunity to participate, and I think Norway is doing the right thing. One thing is political forces in their own country that want to weaken our participation in committed international cooperation. One thing is a country, on the one hand, outside the cooperation structures, which is trying to re-establish the greatness of the former. But it is even more demanding when, in reality, the main country of institutional political order is turning its back on international responsibility and commitments. Because that is really what President Trump is doing. The Canadian Foreign Minister, Chrystia Freeland, said in an interview with The Globe and Mail on June 6 that the United States has left the world, and that Canada and their allies will stand together. Canada will preserve the multilateral institutions and the Western alliance, also in the light of the 'America First' policy. It's an important message to the one we've seen as our most important ally, which I think Norway has to stand behind. It's disturbing when the situation has become so that it's the generals we put our trust in to keep order in Washington. It has been an important principle that Norwegian foreign policy should be anchored in international law and based on internationally committed cooperation. More than ever, it is necessary to play with the political forces that will end up and develop the democratically anchored culture of cooperation developed over the last 70 years in international policy.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-15.ana.ud176e1889	Without resorting to clichés, there is little doubt that the power structures in the world are experiencing fundamental changes. Bonds between countries we used to take for granted are challenged for various reasons, and the uncertainty dominates. In such a situation, it is obvious that Norwegian foreign and security policy cannot remain firm. To the best of our ability, we need to interpret and understand the changes and adapt to the best interests of Norway. Norway has long played a global role that far exceeds our size and importance. It may in some cases be positive for the country, but it can also confront us with foreign policy responsibility in addition to what we can deal with on our own. This means that Norway should be cautious about launching new diplomatic missions. The outcome of the presidential elections in the United States could in the long run have a major impact on cooperation across the Atlantic. The US's new and strong scepticism for international cooperation and supranational commitments is new and confronts all European countries with new and unknown challenges. At the same time, we have to deal with the realities. The United States' expressed wish that the interests of the United States are better protected by partial isolation and renegotiation of international agreements is due to long-term latent currents in the American population. This is not a single event, but a trend that will strengthen in the years to come. Time suggests that most Americans are increasingly related to the cold war, and that new generations are looking forward, not backwards. Demographic changes in both the United States and Europe contribute to our former common identity becoming less clear and the United States' foreign policy priorities are changing as a consequence. In any case, it is absolutely essential that Europe and the United States maintain defence cooperation and that we be able to stand together in our protection of democracy and common values of freedom. If we're going to get this done, it's important that we make NATO more relevant, and that the alliance sticks to the problems it's set to do. NATO shall be a defence and security alliance, in which case the discussions within the alliance must relate to it. Unfortunately, as a member of the Parliamentary Delegation of the Storting to NATO, I have, in part, experienced the opposite, which has been reacted to by American colleagues. Cooperation within NATO faces several challenges. The alliance grows rapidly and has some growing pain. Developments in the authoritarian direction of Turkey can in the long run damage cooperation, and breaking alliances in the war on terror can in the long run make NATO cooperation more demanding. Neither does there exist a definite end to a war against terrorism, which means that an exit strategy does not really exist. It does not necessarily mean that international forces do not make a good and idealistic effort, with the aim of combating barbarism and evil, but at a time to confirm that terror is combated, and that the war is won, will be almost impossible. The war on terrorism also has a different consequence for which European countries were little prepared. Refugees and migration, on grounds of the ongoing wars, are putting European cooperation to the test. Only this year, 200,000 migrants are expected to try to get over the Mediterranean. It is a paradox that, while European countries are sending their young men and women to fight terrorism in several countries in the Middle East, their own young men flee to Europe to seek asylum. There is little doubt that the upsurge of radical Islam is increasing – that is true in all parts of the world. Beast terror, with the aim of killing as many civilians as possible, has become part of the new everyday life in many places. Terror is a form of warfare that is difficult to protect against. If you wish, it would restrict the freedoms of the inhabitants that we cannot or will not accept. The fight against terrorism must therefore start where it finds the ground. Fanatism and terrorism are not due to poverty or unemployment. It is due to the acceptance of hate messages conveyed through Muslim religious communities and not least Quran schools in the Muslim world. Norwegian foreign policy must take this on board, and we must be consistent with who we play with – Saudi Arabia, for example, is not a country Norway in any way should work closely with. If we don't, it's not credible if we raise our voice to other countries' or other groups' violation of human rights. Our peaceful coexistence with Russia has been tested in recent years. It is therefore positive that the government expresses an ambition to improve and further develop our relationship with Russia. Of course, it must not go beyond the international legal order, but at the same time we must assess our neighbourhood in a broad and long-term perspective. It's e.g. infrequently wise to impose penalties without having a realistic objective of when the penalties can be abolished. In a moving world, it is important that Norway join closer to those countries that share our value base. It is therefore positive that the government wants to strengthen the European and Nordic dimension of Norwegian security policy through closer security policy cooperation with selected European allies. This community must naturally also include a common fight against radical Islam and Islamist terrorism. Norwegian foreign policy shall put Norway’s interests first. It is therefore very important that the government actively works to establish good trade relations with new major powers in Asia. In this context, it is important that the government and our export companies play a team, and that through our diplomacy we open doors. In this context, it is very positive that our political contacts and relations with China become normalized. That Asia's major powers, China and India, have caused millions of people out of poverty, is a brunt of dimensions, and it shows how vital it is to have a stable governance and not least an education system that focuses on knowledge and science.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-15.ana.ud176e1919	Norwegian foreign policy begins in Europe. An active European policy has been central to this government. The events in Europe over the last four years have shown that the foreign policy line has been successful, and not least the strategic decision to appoint a separate EU/EEA Minister has proved to be of great importance to Norway. Since February 2014, Europe has been marked by major dramatic, foreign turbulence and growing acute crises. We have experienced Russia's violation of international law, refugees and a wave of migration and the emergence of violent extremism and jihadism. There has been a continuous series of horrific terrorist attacks in cities in the very heart of Europe, now last in the United Kingdom. This, together with low economic growth, has given ground for the emergence of extreme forces and parties on the outer fleas, often with simple answers. We see worrying moves by countries in the EU moving in an anti-democratic direction. We are experiencing a vulnerability that is increasing, and increasing unforeseeableness. The basic values of our European liberal democracies are being challenged – values that the generations before us have fought and that we must ask ourselves if we have taken for granted. Freedom of expression is under pressure in many places. We see sub-currents we haven't seen since the mid-war: a wave of anti-globalization, scepticism to established institutions, isolationism, protectionism and nationalism. These are trends that are increasing at a time when we need the opposite, namely more cooperation. European cooperation is now under test. Crisis management has marked the EU in recent years. EU countries have different approaches. Some want closer integration and more solidarity, while the United Kingdom, our most important ally in Europe, has chosen to break out of the Union through Brexit. The choices made by the British and the Member States will shape Europe for a long time to come. It will require a lot of political leadership, and it will have consequences for Norway. It is therefore important to fence around the EEA Agreement. I am pleased that the government is close to the processes and takes good care of Norwegian interests. It is in Norway’s interest to continue the long lines of Norwegian foreign and security policy. There have been several people here today. At the same time, the situation requires that we adapt policy to the new challenges we face, i.e. that we make choices. A key conclusion of the Communication is that Norway needs to build closer security policy cooperation with the EU and European allies, further develop and create closer links with Germany, the United Kingdom, France and the Netherlands, as well as the Nordic countries. We need to ensure better Norwegian access to the planning and implementation of EU operations, and we need to double the assistance to the Balkans. It is also good that the government will apply for the Norwegian Presidency of the OSCE in 2020, in order to also take responsibility for security policy in Europe. Although the situation is uncertain, we must not, however, lose faith in Europe's future and the robustness of the universal values on which we build our democracies.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-15.ana.ud176e1984	As the rapporteur for the Report Configuration. St. 24 for 2016-2017 – Joint responsibility for the common future – I would like to start by thanking the Committee for its cooperation. This is an important message on a topic that has been much debated this spring. With the Communication, the Government wishes to highlight the course for Norwegian development policy in the future and how development policy and assistance can contribute to the greatest possible reduction of poverty and sustainable development in a changed and demanding international situation. Sustainability targets oblige all countries. Norway will follow-up and will set sustainability targets at the heart of development policy. So this message deals with the international aspect of our relationship with sustainability goals. I therefore believe that it is important to establish that there is a unanimous committee presenting today's position and that a unanimous committee in its comments states that ‘there is a broad cross-political consensus on Norwegian development policy’ and that ‘the overall objectives of aid are and remain to save lives, alleviate distress and contribute to poverty reduction’. And then in this setting, we have some nuances and clarifications that deepen the opinions and views of the different parties. However, the Committee notes that the broad lines of development policy are being pursued. This agreement is precisely what allows Norway to do its long-term work on, for example: education, health, climate and forest activities. Norway should also continue to be champions of international standards and driving rules, and human rights are at the forefront here, as I see it. With this Communication, the government has sought to facilitate the increased use of partnerships with the private sector, civil society, multilateral actors and national authorities in cooperation countries – in order to contribute to poverty reduction and relief of poverty in an increasingly complex world, where the relative importance of assistance is decreasing. No country can develop from the outside. Responsibility for the development of a country and the needs of its inhabitants lies primarily with the authorities of that country. Norwegian assistance must therefore aim at helping developing country authorities to build up state capacity and good services in their own country. Development assistance and assistance are still important in many places, but it must deliver results and have an impact. This is why this government has already started a process of clearer results orientation and more efficiency in aid work. A geographical concentration of aid has been achieved and administrative action has been taken. The number of countries receiving Norwegian assistance and the number of aid agreements have decreased between 2014 and 2016, and in the future, Norwegian bilateral assistance will focus on a limited number of partner countries. The Committee notes – and supports – the effectiveness and improvement of aid management in this way. The Committee also supports the exclusion of humanitarian aid, non-governmental organisations and Norfund from the concentration principle. Assistance is potentially a powerful tool to support the efforts of national authorities to develop their own country. But it is the country's resource base, national policies, trade and economic growth that are most important for poverty reduction. Private sector and private investment are therefore an important driver of economic development. The assistance budget will in future focus on some key areas of action: education, health, business development and job creation, climate, renewable energy, environment and humanitarian action. Action areas are all at the heart of economic growth and poverty reduction. They are interconnected and mutually reinforcing. Gender equality is a cross-cutting issue — and not least, continuing to prioritise education for women is a very important area for us. It is good because in a recent report by UNESCO, the Global Education Monitoring Report, one can unfortunately read that for six years in a row, it is a fact that the total amount of aid for education has been reduced. Aid for training is now far from what is needed to achieve sustainability objective No. 4th@item: inlistbox For example, the United States and the United Kingdom have have been the largest providers in the field of education for several years, but their contributions fell by 11 pst, respectively. and 9 pst. from 2014 to 2015. UNESCO bases its report on new OECD figures and they show that Norway and Germany can be presented as the honorable exceptions, with an increase of 50 pst, respectively. and 34 pst. Norway has also set the need for increased international funding for education on the global agenda. We have a responsibility – and we take responsibility. Then it is also about making the best contribution, as our Prime Minister spoke of when she attended the European Aid Conference in the European Parliament on 7 June, that aid must be set up in such a way as to dampen war and conflict and to support it under sustainable solutions, education, health and job creation. This is precisely because demographic developments in Africa and the Middle East mean that millions of young people enter a labour market that is unable to absorb them each year. The population of Africa is expected to double and now well over two billion by 2050. About 70 pst. the population is less than 30 years of age and the average age is 18. It is estimated that almost 20 million new jobs are needed each year. That's a lot of numbers. The Committee also notes that more than half of Norwegian aid already goes to sub-Saharan Africa and that 73pst. of new investments from Norfund in 2016 were made in Africa. The Committee also notes that ‘important contributions to industrial development take place through Norfund and that the Government proposes to increase support’. The figures tell us that through their investments Norfund has contributed to the creation of nearly 300,000 jobs and the creation of about 300,000 jobs. 700 companies. It's a violent work that needs to be done to prevent lost generations. The government has therefore in its period doubled the investment in education and maintained its commitment to health. Aid for humanitarian aid has increased by 50 pst. We can't do everything, but we can do our part. Consensus has been a topical issue in the aid debate. I could say more about – as time went by – but I would like to briefly remind you that the conclusions of reports on the coherence of government and departments, as well as from other Norwegian and international reports and indices, are that Norwegian policies, with few exceptions, are among the most coherent in the OECD area. Since 2010, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs has had a close dialogue with other departments on the impact of their policies on developing countries. The sustainability objectives reinforce the need for coherence between foreign and domestic policies in order to create development in all countries and secure global common resources. That's a consensus. In the end, I want to do a rare business from this pulpit, namely self-criticism. As rapporteur of the case, I think we could have deepened our view of the «leave no one handicap» of sustainability objectives in a broader way, not least in the light of the fact that this very day, June 15, is a special day. Yes, perhaps we could have said something about the special challenges of the disabled in poor countries, and in areas of war and conflict. There's a billion disabled people in the world, and 80 pst. They live in poor countries. 20 million disabled children do not go to school, 12 million of those over 60 million refugees are disabled, and more than one million disabled people die each year of poverty and malnutrition. The Committee points out in its attitude to the plans to set up a knowledge bank to coordinate and strengthen the work on technical assistance and knowledge transfer – in order to be able to make an effort where Norway has additional prerequisites. We support this. Programmes such as Fish for Development, Oil for Development and Tax for Development are included in these plans — in addition to Norwegian institutions and ombudsman schemes for the promotion of human rights and equality. Norwegian experience with the development of inclusive education, assistance centres, local organisation, user-driven personal assistance and universal design could be actively shared and met with the demand created by sustainability goals in partner countries. But on top of this: when you see the setting, it's still the discrepancy that lies there, to a large extent divides between the Right and the Advance Party on one side and all the other parts on the other side. Several of them already have names. The poverty orientation is pointed out in joint comments from SV, the Labour Party, the Center Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left. The comment taken by the representative Hareide, which concerned the choice of partner countries, has the same character – a long and important note. When it comes to the mechanism of the concentration and view of the vulnerability strategy and its follow-up, all five parties stand together against the government. The same applies to the indication of the need for cohesion reform and how important it will be. It refers to the importance of inequality in development policy. It's hard for me to understand that the Right Party and the Progress Party are not in there. They are not. On the other hand, the Right Party, but not the Progress Party, has entered into another important note, pointing out that the environment and climate should be a central part of Norwegian development policy in the coming years. So the ideological – or value – distinction persists here, as it did in the representative proposals from the Christian People's Party earlier in ours. That's a promise, because in a number of these areas, we can hope to see a revitalization of development policy and a more active government, if there's a government change. I have collaborated with very competent foreign ministers – different, but competent, we are lucky in Norway – with very different but also very competent committee leaders, and a set of competent, central and very cozy politicians in two foreign committees, a couple of them even for two periods. They've taught me an enormous amount, many of these capable people, and we're lucky to have so competent employees to run our foreign policy.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-15.ana.ud176e2605	Several have not asked for the floor on case No. Eight.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-15.ana.ud176e2621	In this proposition, 123 S for 2016-2017, the government asks for the Stortingen approval to launch five new investment projects. There are four new EBA projects: new aviation fuel and supply stock on Guards, different noise measures both inside and outside the base of the Ørland Aviation Station and new hangars for the F-35 aircraft in Orland. It is also not least the approval of the cost framework for a new large material project, namely the acquisition of four new submarines. In addition, there are two other issues asked for the approval of the Storting: multinational cooperation on multi-roll tank and transport aircraft and a change in the basic rule for the Voluntary Shooting Service. The Committee shall adopt a unanimous position on investments and no proposals shall be made in the case. The overall cost framework for the investments we are dealing with here today is over 45 billion. kr. In view of this, given the importance of investments to strengthen Norway's defence capabilities, I believe it is in place to spend some time going a little closer to individual projects. In 2006, Stortinget agreed to an agreement between Norway and the United States on the pre-storage of materials and strengthening of Norway. The supply of fuel is under that agreement one of Norway’s tasks. Guards have an important role, including: in the event of the receipt of allied forces. The planned work on Guards will require large quantities of aviation fuel, which sets high requirements for capacity and availability. As indicated in the proposal, it is necessary to improve the state and capacity of the installations for aviation fuels in Trandellag, both for the purpose of national preparedness and as a result of our alliance obligations. Different storage and location solutions have been evaluated, but since the fuel cannot be stored for a longer period of time, it is a good solution to enter into cooperation with civil aviation, where the Defence builds a tanker that can also supply fuel for the civil operation on the Guards. It is proposed to build a new installation in Muruvik in the municipality of Malvik and a fuel line from the Muruvik plant into the Værne air station. The total cost framework for the project is 309 million. kr. The government is also asking for approval to invest in new noise measures in Ørland. The expansion of Ørland Air Station as the main base for the F-35 aeroplanes involves a doubling of the activity with fighter aeroplanes compared to today, while the F-35 aeroplanes emit more noise than the F-16 aeroplanes. As a consequence of requirements both in the Working Environment Act and in the Municipal Regulatory Plan, it is necessary to implement noise abatement measures both on the Defence’s own building mass inside the Ørland Air Station and on civil property in the noise zones outside the base area itself. The total cost framework for actions on the Defence’s own buildings, redemption and disposal, demolition or securing of buildings outside the base amounts to 1538 million. kr. In a short time, the first F-35 will land on Norwegian soil. It is an investment that will give the entire Norwegian defence a huge operational boost. The decision to lay the main base for the flights to Örland was taken by the Stortinget in 2012, and in 2014 the first investments started. It is a giant plant that rises in the OR and the work is on the way. The station is getting ready to receive the first new F-35 aircraft in November this year. The most recent construction project to be carried out in Ørland is the construction of new hangars. It is proposed to build 12 hangars, each with space for two F-35 fighter planes. A compact solution has been chosen, with the location of the hangars at a short distance from the maintenance and maintenance buildings. This optimises the day-to-day operation and maintenance of the fighter aeroplanes. Hangars must be completed by spring 2020 at the latest to switch from F-16 to F-35 as planned. I Prop. 73 S for 2011–2012 were the assumptions that the fighter aircraft base would only meet the requirements for peacekeeping. This has changed along the way. In the current long-term plan, it was decided that aircraft bases to be used for crisis and armed conflict should be provided with increased protection and protection against attacks compared to the assumptions in the previous long-term plan. With the short alert time now being taken into account, it is important that the base is designed to function as a fully-fledged base in peace, crisis and war from the very first day. An external quality assurance of the project has been carried out and its results underpin the proposed cost framework of a total of 2 266 million. kr. Accommodation and ICT equipment are included. The Committee welcomes the fact that the Ministry of Defence returns to Stortinget with its own case describing a concept of how the base can maintain its functions in peace, crisis and war, and showing how the various measures, such as: air protection, base defence, fuel installations, hangars and other infrastructure shall be optimised in order to ensure optimum operation. Furthermore, the Committee welcomes the fact that the increased costs associated with the implementation of noise abatement measures and the construction of a better-protected hangar solution are within the cost picture of the operation of the Stortinget. 62 S for 2016-2017. Today, Stortinget will also approve the investment framework for the acquisition of new submarines for Defence, cf. decisions taken in connection with the current long-term plan. It's a brand day. The four new submarines that Norway will acquire, with phase-in from the mid-2020s, will strengthen our defence along the coast and in the sea areas, especially in the north. For a maritime nation such as Norway, submarines are one of the most important military capabilities and an important and strategic resource. They can operate hidden over long periods of time over large areas, have great impact through their great influence on maritime military operations and contribute significantly to the defence's deterrence capability. Norway has now entered into strategic cooperation with Germany on this acquisition, which will also include education, training, maintenance, lifetime studies, spare parts, updates and upgrades. Germany and Norway will acquire identical submarines and the production of boats will be coordinated. Such cooperation will reduce operating and maintenance costs and create significant ring-effects for the Norwegian defence industry. It is also worth mentioning that strategic cooperation has been started with Germany, which will allow for the sale of missiles to the same country, further development of missiles and missile technology, and cooperation on a battleline system for both Norwegian and German submarines. Cooperation will be positive for the development of high-tech competences in Norway and for the export potential of Norwegian developed products. The defence industry is an important partner in defence. It is therefore appropriate that the considerations of the Norwegian defence industry, the possibilities for developing competences and increasing international exports of Norwegian defence technology are emphasised in this proposition. The submarine project is quality-secured according to guidelines for large-scale State investment and the quality assurance report supports the main conclusions of the decision-making basis. The total cost framework for the new project is 41,370 million. kr. It is in line with the assumptions of the new long-term defence plan. On that basis, the Committee will endorse the recommended cost framework. Today's submarine infrastructure at Haakonsvern and in Ramsund will be largely reused, but it will be necessary to adapt berths and replace simulators. The Committee welcomes the return of the government to the Stortinget with a recommended maintenance solution proposal for the new submarines in 2019. During the examination of the long-term plan last year, the Stortinget decided that Norway will join the European cooperation project to acquire, operate and use a multinational fleet of multi-roll tank and transport aircraft, MRTT. Air-to-air refuelling is a prerequisite for ensuring effective use of the new fighting aircraft, and there is a high demand capacity in NATO. Multinational MRTT cooperation will provide a better burden-sharing internal to NATO Member States. The costs of Norway’s participation will be covered within the current budgetary framework of the Ministry of Defence. Finally, as regards the proposed changes to the basic rule of the Voluntary Protective Service, which have been made on the initiative of the Voluntary Protective Service itself, the majority of the Committee’s view is that the change, which also opens up membership for non-Norwegian citizens, including young people under 18 years of age, will harmonise the legislation with that of other shooting teams, such as the Norwegian Protective Association and teams and associations associated with Norway’s Sports Association and Others. The Council of State has also addressed various issues related to this in depth in an answer to a question raised by the Committee. With this I will recommend the setting. I would like to thank the Committee and the Council of State for the cooperation in this matter and throughout the great period that we are now putting behind us.

But let me make one comment to what the representative Serigstad Valen said, namely that the frigates became more expensive than planned. They didn't.
