The proposal also contains information on the multinational agreement on multi-rolling tank and transport aircraft and a case relating to the amendment of the statutes of the Voluntary Protective Agency. In June 2012, the Stortinge decided on the location and establishment of the main base for fighter aircraft on Ørland. The first planes come to Ørland in November 2017, if everything goes as planned. With the three F-35-related projects proposed in the proposition, we're putting new pieces in place to provide the Defense with a functional and secure base where we can operate fighter aircraft throughout the spectrum from peace through crisis to armed conflict. Two of the proposals submitted for investment projects shall ensure that the disadvantages of noise emitted by the fighter aircraft are minimised. A number of dwellings outside the base have to be isolated and several properties have to be redeemed and then demolished. It involves pressures for people who may have to leave a place they've lived all their lives. This is something the government takes seriously, and we are therefore looking for good solutions for each household. It is also necessary to implement noise measures on the Defence’s own buildings inside the base. Many people have their daily work on the base, and in order to provide them with the best possible working environment, it is necessary to ensure that the requirements of the law on noise pollution are not exceeded. The new fighter aircraft represent a significant investment for the country. For the time when they are not operating in air operations, it is necessary to establish safe hangar solutions that protect against external influences and minimise the risk of loss. Therefore, an approved project is proposed for the construction of 12 new hangars with space for two aircraft each. Each hangar will be equipped with facilities to prepare the aeroplanes for mission. I would also like to highlight the fourth investment project presented in the proposal. The government is proposing to build a new fuel recovery facility in Muruvik with pipelines to the Værnes Air Station. This provides good opportunities for cooperation with the civil aviation operations on Guards, because there are limitations on how long fuel can be stored before it has to be destroyed. The six Ula-class Ula submarines were put into service between 1989 and 1992 and have an expected technical life expectancy of approx. 30 years. As part of the work on the conceptual studies on a future submarine capacity after 2020, extensive assessments were made of the technical status of the vessels. In line with the conclusions of the studies, the Stortinget approved in 2015 a project to keep existing Ula-class submarines technically operational towards new submarines, which could be delivered from approx. 2025. The government, as the Storting is known, made a basic decision in autumn 2014 to acquire new submarines instead of continuing any extension of life. The government is now submitting the acquisition project for approval. The project is based on committed and long-term marine-to-marine cooperation with Germany, which includes both submarines and other capacities. Cooperation will also include the training, training, operation, maintenance and support of the four new submarines to be acquired by Norway. In addition, cooperation with Germany includes extensive industrial cooperation with Norwegian companies, which will provide large business opportunities for the Norwegian defence industry. Submarines are among the main capabilities of the Defence and are of great importance to our ability to defend Norway's maritime interests. The foremost feature is to be able to operate hidden, over large distances, for a long time and with great impact. It is important that we have now found a strategic partner with which we can build a comprehensive and long-term cooperation. This provides a good starting point for the long-term cooperation relations we need to maintain a credible submarine capability in the future. The government is working with a goal of setting up a contract for new submarines during 2019. It allows phase-in from approx. 2025 and up to 2030, which in turn helps Norway to maintain a continuous submarine capacity when the Ula class is phased out. Norwegian companies are world leaders in some of the technology used in submarines, and the government will use the acquisition as a door opener to ensure the Norwegian defence industry international market access. The acquisition of new submarines is actively being used against international partners to further develop a competent and competitive Norwegian defence industry. The government is working to establish industrial agreements with the ambition that the agreements will collectively ensure the assignment of the Norwegian defence industry to a value equivalent to the acquisition value of new submarines. The proposed proposal proposes to amend a general rule for the Voluntary Sniper Service, DFS. The change will also allow for membership of the DFS for persons under 18 years of age without a permanent residence permit and for persons over 18 years of age with a permanent residence permit. The representative of Tybring-Gjedde thought it was strange that the case was mentioned, but that is because the Stortinget, by a decision of 1892, assumes that the Ministry of Defence must assess the DFS’ application for a change of rules and then submit it to the Stortinget. The size is, of course, free to change this if the Storting wants it, so that you get a greater decoupling between Stortinget and the Voluntary Shooter Service. The proposed regulatory change will harmonise the DFS legislation with the other sports shooting or hunting organisations. Citizenship is not required for membership of other firearms organisations, nor does the Arms Act require citizenship by acquisition of firearms. In conclusion, I would like to say that I am very pleased that the Committee is joining the government's proposal for investment in Defence. Projects are all important contributions to strengthening defence and ensuring its credibility and relevance in a more demanding security policy time.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-15.ana.ud176e3187	As was just said, we're dealing with three things here. It is the industrial communication, a proposal for a representative on a simpler everyday life for small and large enterprises in the country and a proposal for a representative on increased green competitiveness. In general, I want to stick to the setting related to the industry message, for which I have been the case rapporteur. Let me first of all begin by thanking the Committee for a very good cooperation and a clean and good work on this position. Let me also thank all the external actors who have made good sense. This is a setting that bears a great deal of cross-political agreement in many areas. For a time – more specifically one week – that has shown and is going to show a divided nutrition committee, I am pleased that we can also gather on both our own proposals and comments and what the government has presented. This is actually the first industrial announcement the government is promoting and the Stortinge treats over 35 years. So it's more than a generation since the last time we were able to deal with this in detail, back in 1981. At the time, industry had more employees than today, both as a proportion of employed and in absolute terms. On the other hand, the profitability was lower. Productivity was definitely weaker. Industry has changed enormously at this time, and the rate of change is most likely to increase only in the years and decades to come. Fortunately, we have a good starting point for meeting these changes. Although the first 24 hours after the presentation resulted in some criticism from the opposition, I find it gratifying that the criticism was largely silent after industry associations, universities and colleges, research institutes – and not least industry itself – praised a large number of companies and organisations for being concrete, ambitious and full of concrete proposals. I think that shows that we have a adaptive and constructive opposition – and that is in many ways good. The map and the terrain should preferably be in agreement. Norway has a proud industrial history. It is estimated that the development of Norwegian industry began approximately at the late Middle Ages with primitive treatment of iron and wood. From there up to the end of the 1800s, most of it was wood and iron extraction. From the end of the nineteenth century and the beginning of the nineteenth century, our first, internal industrial revolution: the expansion of power. The hydropower laboratory at the technical-natural university of Norway, former NTH, which last week celebrated 100 years, can most likely be called Norway’s most profitable construction ever. By the turn of the century, the work was about 20 p.m. by the population of industry — a number that remained stable for a long time. In the post-war period, we got our second great industrial wave. Shipbuilding, engineering, chemical, electrical engineering and mechanical engineering grew. In 1950, over a quarter of all employees worked in the industry. Until now, industry has been characterised by new technologies, in particular computer technology, which have led to more efficiency and rationalisation. Today work under 10 pst. of the employed in the industry. There is no quick fix or a simple measure that makes industry bigger or stronger tomorrow than yesterday, but there is a set of instruments that have to work together – at least in the Progress Party. These include: on the tax regime, on competence, research and development, on the ability to convert, and on simplification and red tape. When the opposition accuses the government of being so-called business neutral because we believe in more instruments, other than pointing out winners and losing, that's not true. The Industry Communication merges into a wide range of active documents and measures in this period, e.g.: a maritime strategy, a maritime strategy, strengthening, extending and legislating the net pay scheme, the founding plan and the strengthening of Enova. When we cut growth-removing taxes and taxes, the social budget increases by 50 pst., increasing industry-related research and development funds by 50 pst., increasing industry's self-funded R & D activity by more than 10 pst. every year by 50 pst., it is a highly active, relevant and forward-looking business and industrial policy. Nevertheless, I am quite sure that we are going to hear the opposite during the debate here today. This is something the industry itself has worked to get, to get an even better coordinated effort, so that we can get even more out of what is definitely an exciting industry for the future. The earth's population is rising sharply, living standards are rising, more people will need both medicine and medical advice. Therefore, it is close to believing that turnover in the industry today, which is about 50 billion. kr. This year, of which 40 p.m. is based on exports, has a potential for further growth. The transition has become a word that everyone has had to deal with in the last few years. Industry has always been changing and changing – there is nothing new about this. What we're assuming is new now and in the years to come is that the rate of change will accelerate – especially by means of and because of digitisation and robotization. Many workers fear the future and their jobs, and more and more report material restructuring or reorganization of their jobs in recent years. Many jobs will disappear, but our goal must be to replace them with new and more jobs than those that disappear. What is certain is that we need to work even smarter and more productively in the future to be competitive in a global market. Digitalisation must meet offensively and with more instruments. The Government proposes to launch a Digital21 process and to obtain the agreement of an overall committee to it. The aim is to establish a comprehensive and coherent strategy across industries and industries. The test centres for industry, Norsk Katapult, are being rolled out. Facilities for testing new ideas and solutions for Norwegian companies will be crucial. Such «common good financing» must be implemented in order to achieve this, at least in a start-up phase. The focus on enabling technologies – or technologies that prove to be so pervasive that they lead to major changes in society, in good Norwegian – with which the government is running – is also part of the digitalisation strategy. Industrial CO2 emissions have been reduced by about 40 pst. the last 25 years, while other emissions to society have increased over the same period. It shows that we have an offensive industry that adopts and develops technologies that contribute to emissions reduction. The Committee, minus a few parties, points out that climate and environmental requirements must not lead to red figures in the accounts of Norwegian industrial enterprises. In many cases, this is a global market place, and we have not been served by industry flagging out to areas with fewer and lower requirements than we have, due to strange climate and environmental requirements. Furthermore, the Committee is pleased that the government is proposing to continue the CO2 compensation scheme for powerful industry and looks forward to negotiations with the EU in that area. The same applies to the government's Procedural 21 proposal, which also gives the Committee its full support. Knowledge, competence, education and research are important for the development of Norwegian industry and meet the challenges posed by technological development. If you look at Norwegian history and industrial history – from constructing the world’s best and most efficient Viking ships, so that we could travel and trade with the rest of the world, through fishing, mining, to today’s offshore and farming industry – the red thread is especially skilled and innovative professionals, realists and technologists. That's why we've invested extra in mathematics in the last few years. We have put extra emphasis on skilled workers, vocational correction of education and training, apprenticeship grants and apprentices in public tenders. Norway has a strong institute sector, and for industry in particular, it is the technical-industrial ones that are important and that help to convert and renew Norwegian business. This has been a very good job for four years, which has already produced good results.

They need an increased focus on innovation, research and development and TaxFUNN: we have done something about that. We've increased our bet on it. There's a record bet in those areas. They need increased awareness and, not least, real science is referred to as incredibly important. In a state where industry is strong, vocational training is extremely important. These are exactly the areas that this government is betting on. Growth-promoting tax havens, which are clearly a four-word on the red-green side: yes, they work. When the company Brunvoll can keep 10 million. kr more every year, that means that they can use that money for something sensible, to create new jobs, to secure existing jobs. That's how it works. It's no more complicated than that. You don't need a research project to figure this out. It's just asking those who have their shoes on and who know where they're pressing. The point is that I think it's done very well. We have an active industrial policy. When someone's talking about nutrition neutrality, that's the opposite we're dealing with. We have a marine strategy, a tourism message, a maritime strategy, a business start-up plan, we strengthen the net pay system, we strengthen Enova, and we are now presenting an industrial report – all this is to build up under the competitive advantages of Norway.
